## Page 1


Weakly turbulent dynamics on Schwarzschild–AdS black hole spacetimes
Christoph Kehle∗1 and Georgios Moschidis†2
1Massachusetts Institute of Technology, Department of Mathematics,
Building 2, 77 Massachusetts Avenue, Cambridge, MA 02139, United States of America
2École Polytechnique Fédérale de Lausanne, Institute of Mathematics,
MA B1 595, CH-1015 Lausanne, Switzerland
April 13, 2026
Abstract
In the presence of confinement, small-data solutions to nonlinear dispersive equations can exhibit
a gradual energy transfer from low to high frequencies, a mechanism driving the emergence of weakly
turbulent dynamics. We show that such a forward energy transfer, manifested as arbitrary inflation
of higher order Sobolev norms, occurs for small-data solutions of a quasilinear cubic wave equation on
the Schwarzschild–AdS black hole exterior with Dirichlet conditions at infinity, for generic values of
the mass parameter. This result is motivated by the question of nonlinear stability or instability of
Schwarzschild–AdS as a solution to the Einstein vacuum equations, but the strategy of proof applies to a
broader class of backgrounds exhibiting stable trapping of null geodesics. As an application, we obtain
the analogous norm inflation statement on R × S3
+ for generic perturbations of the round metric on the
hemisphere S3
+ preserving the trapping structure at the boundary.
∗kehle@mit.edu
†georgios.moschidis@epfl.ch
arXiv:2604.12118v1  [gr-qc]  13 Apr 2026

Introduction
The phenomenon of turbulence, characterized by the presence of an energy flux between different scales
in systems far from thermodynamic equilibrium [82, 61], is ubiquitous in the world of nonlinear dispersive
equations. In cases when the nonlinear interactions are appropriately weak, individual modes evolve almost
linearly at short timescales, with the progressive exchange of energy among modes mediated by resonant
interactions. The theory of weak (or wave) turbulence was developed in the 60s as a unifying framework
to describe such processes in a broad class of systems modeled by Hamiltonian PDEs, from oceanic gravity
waves to nonlinear optics systems [43, 80, 79, 81, 57, 61].
The emergence of weakly turbulent dynamics in systems of finite energy typically involves a mechanism of
confinement at the linear level: In bounded geometries (e.g. on compact manifolds, in cavities with reflecting
boundaries) or in settings with strong trapping (e.g. spacetimes admitting stably trapped null geodesics,
in the case of wave-type equations), linear perturbations persist as long-lived oscillatory modes. If certain
resonance conditions are satisfied, these oscillatory modes allow weakly nonlinear interactions to accumulate
over time, enabling the secular transfer of energy to progressively higher frequencies, a process known as a
forward (or direct) energy cascade, signified by the inflation of higher order Sobolev norms of the solution.
On the other hand, strong dispersion at the linear level obstructs a direct cascade: rapid decay of linear
perturbations prevents nonlinear effects from accumulating and the nonlinear evolution remains close to the
linear one.
The Einstein vacuum equations
Ric[g] −1

(1.1)
is a prominent example of a geometric nonlinear system of PDEs of hyperbolic nature. As such, given a
stationary spacetime solution with confining geometry, one would expect its nonlinear perturbations to exhibit
weakly turbulent dynamics. In the case of a negative cosmological constant Λ (without loss of generality, we
set Λ = −3 for the rest of the paper), the Schwarzschild–Anti-de Sitter (AdS) exterior spacetime

1.2
A test case for weak turbulence on Schwarzschild–AdS
The nonlinear initial–boundary value problem (1.3) can be considered as a testbed for the study of weakly
turbulent phenomena in the dynamics of gravitational perturbations on the Schwarzschild–AdS exterior
spacetime. From this perspective, let us briefly discuss the reasons motivating the particular choice of the
nonlinearity N (3)[ϕ] in (1.3).
1.2.1
A model for the vacuum Einstein equations
The well-posedness of the initial–boundary value problem for (1.1) in the asymptotically Anti-de Sitter setting
was first considered by Friedrich [31], who established the existence and geometric uniqueness of solutions for
a broad class of boundary conditions defined in terms of the conformal geometry of I. This class included
a unique Dirichlet-type condition, corresponding to the prescription of a conformally flat geometry on I
(for the description of a geometric Neumann boundary condition leading to a well-posed problem, see the
recent work [74]). His proof was based on the construction of a gauge tied to the conformal structure of the
spacetimes under consideration.
For the corresponding problem expressed in the harmonic gauge, well-posedness of the Dirichlet initial–
boundary value problem was established by [29] (based on the earlier work [38] in the Riemannian setting).
Denoting by ˜g = gAdS the reference AdS metric

Understanding whether a direct energy cascade appears in the evolution of solutions to (1.9) requires
a careful analysis of the resonant structure of the nonlinear terms. The quadratic terms N (2)
Ein satisfy the
so-called weak null condition, uncovered in [56], which prohibits certain types of self-interactions in the
solution. In the case of small data solutions to systems of nonlinear wave equations on R3+1, it is known (see
[73, 70]) that quadratic nonlinearities satisfying certain algebraic conditions (which include the classical null
condition [14, 54]) can be “transformed” away in the context of their effect on the nonlinear dynamics. It
is, therefore, not unreasonable to expect that a similar transformation might exist for the Einstein system,
precluding the quadratic terms from contributing to the resonant system. On that basis, in our pursuit of a
scalar model for (1.9), we decided to drop the quadratic terms altogether,1 arriving at a quasilinear cubic
nonlinear equation of the form

1.2.2
Pure power nonlinearities: An obstruction to energy transfer
Attempting to set up a scalar model for the study of the weakly turbulent properties of the Einstein system
(1.9), a natural first choice in place of (1.3) would be the simplest possible nonlinear wave equation with
cubic nonlinearity, namely
□gM ϕ = N (3)
pow[ϕ] .= |ϕ|2ϕ
(or more generally N (p)
pow[ϕ] = |ϕ|p−1ϕ for p ≥3). As it turns out, however, these nonlinearities have additional
structure that does not allow the proof strategy for Theorem 1 to apply.
While the quasilinear term N (3)[ϕ] in (1.4) and the semilinear term N (3)
pow[ϕ] = |ϕ|2ϕ both allow for the
same set of temporal resonances (arithmetic progressions of frequencies) and the same reduction of the system
to the system of resonant interactions of dominant modes (with all error terms controlled in exactly the same
way), the corresponding resonant systems differ fundamentally in the structure of the interaction coefficients.
For a resonant triad of modes, the effective dynamics of the amplitudes are governed by a system of ODEs
determined by these coefficients. In the case of the semilinear power nonlinearity N (3)
pow[ϕ], the symmetries of
the interaction coefficients lead to a system that is linearly stable. Specifically, the energy flows between
modes in an oscillatory manner rather than exhibiting the exponential growth required for a forward energy
cascade on the relevant timescale. In contrast, the quasilinear structure of (1.4), involving derivatives of
ϕ, breaks this symmetry. This results in interaction coefficients that allow for linear instability, creating a
mechanism where energy is consistently pumped from low to high frequencies rather than merely exchanged.
See already Remark 1.15 for a more detailed discussion of this point.
Remark 1.3. As will become clear from the outline of the proof in Section 1.5, the strategy of our proof
carries over to the case when N (3)[ϕ] is replaced by any cubic nonlinearity almost verbatim, with the exception
of the analysis of the resonant 3 × 3 system. The coefficients of that system (which can be viewed as spatial
overlap integrals of the dominant mode eigenfunctions with weights determined by the nonlinearity) are
sensitive to the precise choice of the nonlinear terms. For instance, dropping the r−6 coefficient from N (3)[ϕ]
results in a dominant system which does not exhibit norm inflation in the required timescale. See Remark 1.16.
1.3
Weak turbulence and energy transfer in nonlinear dispersive PDE
The emergence of energy cascades in the evolution of weakly interacting nonlinear waves has been the object
of extensive studies in the fields of water waves, plasma physics and nonlinear optics, beginning already in the
’60s (see e.g. [43, 78, 79]). During that decade, it was heuristically understood that, for solutions to a wide
class of Hamiltonian PDEs, the transfer of energy between different scales gives rise to dynamics resembling
those associated to hydrodynamic turbulence. In contrast to the dynamics of fluids, however, in the case of
dispersive PDEs with weak nonlinearities, the energy flow is mediated by resonant nonlinear interactions
among modes evolving in an approximately linear fashion at short timescales, inspiring the name weak or
wave turbulence for the theory describing the associated phenomena (see [61]).
For a given nonlinear dispersive PDE, the length-scale L associated with the spatial domain and the size
of the initial data determine an effective timescale Teff of interaction, at which the nonlinear nature of the
equations dominates the dynamics. Using the scaling properties of the underlying equations (in the case
of a homogeneous nonlinearity), one may transform the study of high-frequency interactions on domains
with L ∼1 to interactions of bounded frequencies in the large-box limit L →+∞. In this limit, different
8


## Page 9


asymptotic relations between the domain size L and the timescale Teff (or, equivalently, the size of the rescaled
initial data) are expected to yield different asymptotic dynamics. In the case when the size of the initial
data is not “too” small, the gaps between the time frequencies of the modes (these gaps vanish in the limit
L →∞) are narrow compared to T −1
eff and the linear spectrum can be considered to be effectively continuous.
In this regime of so-called kinetic wave turbulence (see [61]), well-prepared randomized initial data formally
give rise to a nonlinear kinetic equation governing the evolution of the (averaged) energy profile in phase
space ([32, 80, 78, 79, 2], with a similar kinetic description appearing already in the study of phonons in
anharmonic crystals by Peierls [65]). Stationary solutions of these kinetic models are characterized by energy
spectra which qualitatively resemble those observed in the turbulent motion of a classical fluid (see [80, 81]).
On the other hand, in the case when the spectral gaps are large compared to T −1
eff (i.e. for sufficiently small
initial data), the dynamics are dominated by exact resonances and the evolution is not expected to admit an
effective kinetic description; this is sometimes referred to as the regime of discrete wave turbulence (see [61,
52, 51] and references therein) and is the setting relevant to Theorem 1.3 We will not provide a representative
survey of the (vast) literature on weak turbulence theory, but instead refer the reader to [81, 28, 57, 62, 61].
A rigorous derivation of the general picture painted above remains elusive to this day. However, questions
on the emergence of energy cascades in the dynamics of nonlinear dispersive PDEs have attracted considerable
attention from the mathematical community in the past three decades, with Bourgain’s expository work [7]
being particularly influential in that direction. There, Bourgain posed the question of whether the cubic
defocusing nonlinear Schrödinger equation
i∂tu −∆u = −|u|2u
(1.12)
on the torus Td with d ≥2 admits, for any Sobolev index s > 1, solutions with ∥u[0]∥Hs ≪1 and such that
∥u[t]∥Hs
t→+∞
−−−−→+∞, signifying an infinite energy cascade as t →+∞. This question still remains open;
however, in a major breakthrough, Colliander, Keel, Staffilani, Takaoka and Tao [15] constructed, for any
s > 1 and ϵ > 0, solutions to (1.12) on T2 with ∥u[0]∥Hs < ϵ and such that ∥u[T]∥Hs > 1
ϵ at some time
T > 0. This was the first proof of a direct energy transfer to arbitrarily large frequencies for small amplitude
nonlinear waves in any setting and sparked a burst of activity on related questions: [41, 44] obtained similar
results for modifications of (1.12), while [40] constructed solutions to (1.12) undergoing norm inflation with
better control of the timescale of growth (for data with small energy but relatively large initial Hs norm); [34]
obtained results analogous to [15] for the completely integrable cubic Szegö equation in 1d; [67] established
the amplification of the Hs norm of solutions to a 1d nonlinear wave equation by an arbitrary factor (with the
Hs norm of the solution, however, remaining ≪1 on the whole time interval). In the case of a non-compact
domain, [42] answered in the affirmative Bourgain’s question when T2 is replaced by R × T2; in that case,
the slow decay at the linearized level due to the dispersive direction helped to isolate the resonant dynamics
from the rest of the evolution, allowing for a global approximation of the evolution by the resonant dynamics
(see also [66, 4, 35]). We should also mention Bourgain’s earlier work [8] where, for a carefully spectrally
designed nonlinear wave equation on the real line, he showed polynomial growth in time for higher order
Sobolev norms.
Remark 1.4. The question proposed by Bourgain (as well as all the results mentioned above and the results
of the current work) fall into the setting of discrete wave turbulence discussed earlier, which has been less
extensively studied in the physics literature. On the other hand, in the regime of kinetic wave turbulence, the
past few years have witnessed remarkable advances towards a rigorous justification of the wave kinetic theory:
In the recent breakthrough [21, 22] (see also [10, 75]), Deng and Hani derived the wave kinetic equation as
an effective statistical description (in the limit L →+∞) of the dynamics of the NLS equation (1.12) on
the torus Td
L, d ≥3, for the full range of scaling laws between the box dimension L and the strength of the
nonlinear interactions at which the theory of continuous wave turbulence is expected to be valid: In terms of
the timescale Teff ∼L2γ, this range corresponds to γ ∈(0, 1]. Note, however, that the initial data regime
where the kinetic wave theory is expected to describe the dynamics of (1.12) is strictly disjoint from that
associated to Bourgain’s question: Upon rescaling back to a torus of size ∼1 (setting u(x, t) →Lu(Lx, L2t)),
the initial data associated to this range of scaling laws have size ≳1 and the interactions take place at
timescales ≲1.
3The intermediate regime, in which the spectral gaps are at the same order as T −1
eff , is referred to as the regime of mesoscopic
turbulence [61, 51].
9


## Page 10


Our main result (namely Theorem 1) should be viewed as the analog of the result of [15] in the setting
of the quasilinear wave equation (1.3) on Schwarzschild–AdS spacetime; Theorem 2 provides the analogous
statement on R × S3
+ equipped with the family of metrics −dt2 + hM
S3
+. Some of the key differences between
our setting and that of earlier works on (discrete) weakly turbulent dynamics can be summarized as follows:
1. Quasilinear nature of the nonlinearity. In contrast to the setting of previous works (with the exception
of the very recent work [55]), the nonlinear equation that we study, namely (1.3), is quasilinear rather
than semilinear. The quasilinear nature of (1.3) features prominently in the analysis of the error terms
in Section 7. Interestingly, our proof strategy does not provide Sobolev norm growth in the case of
semilinear power nonlinearities of the type Npow[ϕ] = |ϕ|p−1ϕ, see Section 1.2.2 for a more detailed
discussion.
2. Equation of wave type. Equation (1.3) is a 2nd order wave-type equation rather than a Schrödinger-type
one. As far as we know, this is the first higher order norm inflation result for such an equation
in spatial dimension higher than 1. In the case of a Schrödinger-type equation, 4-term (nontrivial)
resonant interactions can only transfer energy from one scale to another one of comparable size; hence
energy transferred from low to high frequencies has to gradually traverse the whole intermediate
frequency spectrum. On the contrary, for a wave-type operator, 4-term resonant interactions can
involve frequencies of arbitrary ratios (we exploit this fact in our selection of the resonant modes, see
Section 4.1).4
3. Curved (non-compact) domain and stable trapping. Another novel aspect of the setting of our work is
that the underlying geometry (namely Schwarzschild–AdS spacetime) is curved, being, in particular,
non-compact in the spatial directions; in the region where the interactions are localized the longest in
time, namely near I, the spatial geometry resembles the near-equatorial geometry of the round half
sphere S3
+ (see Section 1.6 for more details on the relation between the asymptotically AdS geometries
and that of R × S3
+). The latter fact is reflected in the existence of stably trapped null geodesics near
the conformal boundary I. In view of the non-compactness in the spatial directions of the domain (and,
in particular, the fact that linear solutions decay by gradually dispersing through the event horizon),
we cannot use normal mode linear solutions to obtain an ansatz for a solution to (1.3). However, in
view of the stable trapping near I, we can construct well-localized quasi-modes instead.
Unlike previous works (which considered a flat background), the curvature of the underlying geometry
makes keeping track of the spatial interactions of those quasi-modes more challenging. For instance, in
the case of the flat torus Td, where the spatial profile of linear modes for the wave (or Schrödinger)
equation is given by Ek = eik·x (k ∈Zd, the orthogonal projection Pk(Ek1 ¯Ek2Ek3) onto Ek is nontrivial
only when k = k1 −k2 + k3; there is no similar statement in the case of a curved geometry. Instead, for
the evaluation of such projections of quasimodes in our setting, we have to rely on careful estimates on
the large frequency asymptotics of the corresponding eigenfunctions. Moreover, the spatial profiles of
the linear quasimodes that remain localized near I do not form a complete basis in L2; therefore, any
decomposition of a solution to (1.3) into a sum of such modes with slowly evolving amplitudes (such as
(1.28)) must necessarily involve a non-trivial error term, and controlling that term requires taking into
consideration the global geometry of the spacetime.
4. Supercriticality. The nonlinear wave equation (1.3) has the same scaling properties as the Einstein
equations and, in particular, is supercritical with respect to the (conserved) H1 energy (1.11). Therefore,
the existence of the solution up until the time t = T1 cannot be obtained as in the case of (1.12) on T2.
In order to obtain this long-time existence result, we have to carefully exploit a certain quantitative
non-resonant structure in the spectrum as well as energy estimates associated to a novel helical vector
field adapted to our construction. Note that establishing that all lower order norms remain small in
this time interval (as part of the proof of the existence of a solution) is crucial in distinguishing an
energy cascade statement from a blow-up scenario, in which lower order norms are also inflated.
4This non-local nature (in terms of scales) of the energy transfer associated to wave-type equations, as opposed to other
dispersive PDEs, is a well-known fact, see already [78].
10


## Page 11


We should remark that, as in the case of [15], Theorem 1 does not provide information about the behavior
of Sobolev norms beyond the time t = T1. In particular, in both settings, Bourgain’s question remains open.
Unlike [42] where, on R×T2, solutions to the linear equation decay at a t−1
2 rate, in our setting the dispersion
due to the event horizon is too weak (leading to a merely logarithmic decay rate at the linearized level);
thus, controlling the difference between the resonant dynamics and the true solution for longer timescale is a
much more challenging problem. In the energy-supercritical setting of (1.3) studied here, there is also the
possibility of a finite-time singularity formation in the evolution of smooth initial data. This motivates the
following question concerning the ultimate fate of the solutions constructed in Theorem 1:
Do the solutions constructed in Theorem 1 exist for all time or do they form a singularity at
some finite time Tblowup > T1? If the former is true, do higher order Sobolev norms blow up
asymptotically as t∗→∞?
1.4
The stability problem for the Schwarzschild–AdS exterior spacetime
The question of whether the Einstein vacuum equations (1.1) (which our scalar model (1.3) aims to simulate)
exhibit weakly turbulent dynamics on the exterior of a Schwarzschild–AdS black hole is intimately connected
to the problem of nonlinear stability of those spacetimes as solutions to (1.1). Let us briefly review the
current status of this problem and explain how our result fits into this context.
1.4.1
Instability of Anti-de Sitter spacetime
The maximally symmetric solution of the vacuum Einstein equations (1.1) with negative cosmological constant
Λ = −3 is Anti-de Sitter spacetime (R3+1, gAdS), with gAdS given by (1.6) (which is formally obtained from
(1.2) for M = 0). This spacetime is conformal to the half cylinder (R × S3
+, −dt2 + gS3
+). Therefore, imposing
reflecting boundary conditions at conformal infinity I = R × S3
+ (see Section 1.2.1 for a brief discussion
about such boundary conditions) introduces confinement in the evolution of perturbations of gAdS, with the
nonlinear nature of (1.1) raising the prospect of an energy cascade emerging in this setting.
In 2006, Dafermos–Holzegel [19] proposed the following conjecture:
The AdS instability conjecture. There exist arbitrarily small perturbations of Anti-de Sitter
spacetime solving (1.1) with reflecting boundary conditions at I which, after sufficiently long
time, lead to the emergence of a trapped surface (signifying, in particular, the creation of a black
hole). Thus, AdS spacetime is nonlinearly unstable.
The picture proposed by the above conjecture can be viewed as a geometric statement of an energy cascade,
since, in light of Thorne’s hoop conjecture [76], the formation of a trapped surface is expected to be possible
only when sufficient “energy” is concentrated at a small enough scale.
A plethora of numerical and heuristic works have been devoted to the study of the conjecture in the class
of spherically symmetric solutions of the Einstein equations coupled to matter fields [23, 9, 25, 26, 58, 1, 39,
27]. This flurry of activity was initiated by the seminal work of Bizon–Rostworowski [6], where the authors
studied spherically symmetric perturbations of AdS for the Einstein–Klein-Gordon system


with α = 0 and with Dirichlet conditions at I. Noting that the spectrum of the linear operator is a rescaling
of Z (and hence contains infinitely long arithmetic progressions of temporal frequencies), they conjectured
that the instability is driven by a mechanism of resonant interactions of spherically symmetric modes,
leading to weakly turbulent dynamics, at least at the early stages of the instability. That mechanism was
further explored in [16, 17, 5]. At the same time, the existence of special perturbations of AdS leading to
quasiperiodic solutions (not collapsing, in particular, into black holes) has been investigated both numerically
and heuristically [58, 1, 39, 27]. A rigorous construction of periodic small amplitude solutions for nonlinear
models of (1.13) was carried out in [12, 13], with their stability at exponential timescales being explored in
[11]. For numerical and heuristic works outside spherical symmetry, see [49, 24, 72].
11


## Page 12


While the AdS instability conjecture remains open in the case of the vacuum equations, it has been shown
to hold in the case of Einstein–null dust and Einstein–massless Vlasov systems in spherical symmetry, at
least within classes of initial data of relatively low regularity [59, 60]. However, a rigorous construction of
perturbations which are small in a high regularity class and exhibit the weakly turbulent behavior predicted
by Bizon–Rostworowski in any matter model is still open.
1.4.2
The case of Schwarzschild–AdS: Quasimodes and resonant interactions
Determining the ultimate fate of the black hole spacetimes postulated by the AdS instability conjecture—
deciding, in particular, whether these solutions asymptotically settle down to stationary black holes—appears
to be at present a highly intractable problem. Understanding the dynamics of these spacetimes would involve
at first understanding the global nonlinear stability properties of stationary asymptotically AdS black holes,
such as the Schwarzschild–AdS exterior spacetime (M(M)
ext , gM).
In the case of the spherically symmetric Einstein–Klein-Gordon system (1.13) with Dirichlet boundary
conditions at I, it was shown by Holzegel–Smulevici [46] that perturbations of Schwarzschild–AdS decay to 0
at an exponential rate (under the technical restriction α ≥2 in (1.13)). Such a strong decay rate is consistent
with the fact that radial null geodesics (along which high-frequency spherically symmetric perturbations are
expected to propagate) fall through the black hole horizon after at most one reflection off conformal infinity
I.
Outside spherical symmetry, however, the stability problem becomes more delicate, owing to the presence
on (M(M)
ext , gM) of stably trapped null geodesics that are reflected infinitely many times off I but never approach
the horizon. These null geodesics have the capacity of supporting long-lived perturbations, as reflected on the
existence of solutions ϕ to the linear Klein–Gordon equation with Dirichlet boundary conditions

Remark 1.5. An alternative approach to establish a logarithmic lower bound for the decay rate of generic
solutions to (1.14) is by studying, in place of quasimodes, families of quasinormal modes, namely functions
of the form (1.15) which are exact solutions of (1.14) with infalling boundary conditions on the horizon
and with a radial profile concentrated in the region r ≫1. In this case, the quasinormal mode frequency
is necessarily complex valued, reflecting the dispersion through the event horizon. Quasinormal modes on
Schwarzschild–AdS have been constructed by Gannot [33]; their frequencies satisfy

The slow decay rate of generic solutions ϕ to the linear problem (1.14) on Schwarzschild–AdS (and, more
generally, on the Kerr–AdS black hole exteriors with parameters satisfying the Hawking–Reall bound) and, in
particular, the exponentially long (in terms of their frequency) lifespan of high-frequency linear quasimodes,
led Holzegel and Smulevici [45] to propose the following nonlinear instability conjecture:
Instability of Kerr–AdS conjecture (Section 1.4 of [45]). The family of Kerr–AdS spacetimes
is nonlinearly unstable for generic perturbations.
Any attempt of proving (or disproving) the above conjecture should necessarily involve an investigation of
the resonant nature of the nonlinear interactions of the long-lived linear quasimodes. Fixing, for definiteness,
a model nonlinear equation with a nonlinearity of cubic type (such as (1.3), for instance), a quartet of
quasimodes will be said to be in temporal resonance if their combined interaction through the nonlinearity is
not out of phase, i.e.
±ωn1,ℓ1 ± ωn2,ℓ2 ± ωn3,ℓ3 ± ωn4,ℓ4 ≈0
(1.18)
(with the size of the allowed error on the right hand side being smaller than the inverse of the effective
timescale of interaction). The existence of quartets of quasimodes of arbitrarily large frequencies which
are temporally (but also spatially!) resonant opens the gate for a nonlinear transfer of energy between the
corresponding frequency scales and can thus be a first indication of nonlinear instability (though in itself is
not a guarantee of an instability, since for energy transfer to occur the resonant system must in addition
exhibit an appropriate instability; see Remark 1.15).
The study of the resonant properties of the spectrum of (1.14) on a broad class of stationary and
asymptotically AdS spacetimes (including Schwarzschild–AdS) was initiated in [25]. There, the authors

never be satisfied if ℓ≫1 (and the same is true for the resonant condition associated to a nonlinearity of any
fixed homogeneous order); these quasimodes are quantitatively non-resonant. The absence of the resonant
energy transfer mechanism for the class of lowest-lying quasimodes, as well as the fact that such linear
quasimodes have an exponentially long but nevertheless finite timescale of nonlinear interaction, motivated
[25] to suggest that, contrary to the conjecture of [45] stated earlier, the Kerr–AdS family might in fact be
nonlinearly stable.
The main result of the present paper (namely Theorem 1) points in the opposite direction: Our proof of
an instability for the nonlinear equation (1.3) is built upon the observation that, once all modes with radial
overtone n ≫1 are taken into account, then a resonant structure emerges in the linear spectrum, at least for
a generic set of mass parameters M > 0. This is a consequence, in particular, of the refinement (1.35) of the
formula (1.19). See Section 1.5 below for more details.
1.4.3
Weakly turbulent instability for the Einstein vacuum equations
Based on Theorem 1 and the discussion above (as well as the fact that (1.3) was selected as a model for
the Einstein vacuum equations, see Section 1.2.1), it is natural to conjecture that an analogous instability
of weakly turbulent nature should occur for the full Einstein vacuum equations on the Schwarzschild–AdS
exterior with reflecting boundary conditions at I. In particular, a “maximalistic” version of this expectation
could be formulated as follows (refining the statement of the instability conjecture of [45]):
Conjecture 1.6 (Nonlinear instability of Schwarzschild–AdS). For any s ∈N, there exists an open and
dense set JEVE
s
⊂(0, +∞) of black hole masses M with the following property: There exists a one-parameter
family of asymptotically AdS initial data (γϵ, Kϵ) which converge in the Hs topology to the Schwarzschild–AdS
initial data (γ0, K0) of mass M such that their evolution under the vacuum Einstein equations with reflecting
boundary conditions at I leads to the creation of a trapped surface in the region far from the background event
horizon.
In light of the hoop conjecture, the emergence of a trapped surface should be interpreted as an indication
of the growth of the scale invariant size of the solution. In the case of the model problem (1.3) studied in
Theorem 1, such a statement would correspond to the growth of the H

Remark 1.8. In the numerical work [30] on scalar perturbations of very slowly rotating Kerr–AdS, the
authors also observed a nonlinear instability associated to mode interactions. The perturbations in [30]
are however of a different nature and are not “small” in the sense of Theorem 1. In particular, our weak
turbulence mechanism in Theorem 1 occurs at much later timescales than those instabilities observed in [30].
Remark 1.9. Another class of spacetimes exhibiting stable trapping are ultracompact neutron stars:
horizonless solutions possessing a photon sphere. In [53] it was shown that linear waves on such backgrounds
decay only inverse-logarithmically. Recent numerical studies [3, 71] further indicate that this slow decay
indeed gives rise to an instability of turbulent nature. We expect that the mechanism and results of the
present paper likewise apply to quasilinear wave equations on such ultracompact objects; see also the recent
review [68] and references therein.
Remark 1.10. In [3], the authors study numerically the cubic nonlinear wave equations on spacetimes
with stable trapping and propose the following scenario for the late-time dynamics of “generic” gravitational
perturbations: While higher order Sobolev norms are inflated as a result of an energy cascade, the energy
of the perturbation should spread out in a homogeneous fashion among the stably trapped modes. As a
consequence of this scenario, energy should not accumulate in a region of small diameter, and hence (in view
of the hoop conjecture) trapped surface formation in that region should not be possible for such “generic”
perturbations. However, the proof of Theorem 1 seems to be at odds with this picture: In the time interval
t ∈[0, T1] where the energy transfer takes place, the solution ϕ remains localized mostly at spherical harmonics
with ℓ= |m|, and the same is true for an open set of initial data around the given ones. This is a consequence
of the fact that the vast majority of the frequencies in the linear spectrum are quantitatively non-resonant
with respect to the initially excited frequency triad, and hence homogenization of the solution cannot be
achieved at the same timescale at which energy is transferred through the resonant interactions. As a result,
we believe that, in the statement of Conjecture 1.6, the space of initial data leading to the creation of a
trapped surface in the far away region should contain an open neighborhood of the stated one-parameter
family.5
1.4.4
Stability of Kerr–AdS for analytic perturbations
In our main result Theorem 1, we measure regularity in Sobolev norms and show growth of these norms as a
consequence of a forward energy cascade. A natural question is whether there exists a regularity threshold
beyond which such an energy transfer ceases to exist; a natural class of data to search for such a threshold is
that of real-analytic initial data, as suggested already by the late time dynamics of analytic solutions to the
linear Klein–Gordon equation (1.14) on Schwarzschild–AdS.

for every ε > 0 and all τ ≥0, where c2 > 0 is the constant from (1.17) and the constant implicit in ≲ε

ρ. In particular, by taking ρ large, one obtains arbitrarily fast inverse-polynomial
decay for real analytic data.
Switching our attention to the nonlinear setting, the above observation would imply that, in the analytic
category, high-frequency linear modes are damped sufficiently rapidly for the nonlinear interactions not
to have sufficient time to alter the dynamics. Consequently, analytic data with sufficiently large radius
of analyticity may avoid a forward energy cascade and instead yield global-in-time decay without weak
turbulence for (1.3). This picture is consistent with the numerical results of [18] for gravitational perturbations
of Schwarzschild–AdS in 5D, where small smooth data that are analytic in the angular variables exhibit
late-time decay and no evidence of a turbulent cascade on the time scales probed.
Motivated by the above discussion, we propose the following stability statement:
Conjecture 1.11 (Stability of Kerr–AdS for analytic perturbations). There exists a radius of analyticity
ρ0 > 0 such that the following holds. For every Kerr–AdS parameters (M, a) satisfying the Hawking–Reall
bound (with M > 0) and every 0 < ϵ ≪1, there exists δ = δ(M, a, ϵ, ρ0) > 0 with the following property: For
any real-analytic, asymptotically Kerr–AdS initial data (γ0, K0) for (1.1) satisfying
∥(γ0, K0) −(γM,a, KM,a)∥ρ ≤δ
for some ρ ≥ρ0, where ∥·∥ρ is a geometrically defined real analytic norm of the form (1.20) and (γM,a, KM,a)
are the Kerr–AdS initial data, we have:
1. The corresponding maximal Cauchy development (M, g) of (γ0, K0) with reflecting boundary conditions
at I has complete conformal infinity and the black hole exterior region is C2 close to the background
Kerr–AdS exterior.
2. There exist Kerr–AdS parameters (M ′, a′) with |(M ′, a′) −(M, a)| < ϵ such that, in an appropriate
gauge, the black hole exterior of (M, g) asymptotically settles down to the Kerr–AdS exterior with
parameters (M ′, a′) at an inverse–polynomial rate:
∥(g(t) −gM ′,a′, K(t) −KM ′,a′)∥Hs(Σt)×Hs−1(Σt) ≲s ϵ (1 + t)−p
for some p > 1 and any s > 0.
1.5
Outline of the proof of Theorem 1
In this section, we will lay out a detailed description of the main ideas involved in the proof of Theorem 1.
1.5.1
The linear wave equation on Schwarzschild–AdS: From trapped quasimodes to normal

Given the above ansatz for ϕ, the proof of Theorem 1 consists of the following steps:
19


## Page 20


1. We first identify a dense set of Schwarzschild–AdS mass parameters M ∈(0, +∞) for which the
linear boundary value problem (1.21) enjoys the following spectral property: Its normal modes ϕk
corresponding to the (carefully chosen) dominant frequency parameters KD = {k-1, k0, k1} satisfy a set
of spatial and temporal resonant conditions, while at the same time being resonantly “separated” from
the modes corresponding to the non-dominant parameters KND.
Remark 1.13. Note that Theorem 1 asks for an open and dense set of mass parameters M for which
norm inflation holds. However, openness follows readily using a soft Cauchy stability statement; see
Section 1.5.7.
2. The system of ODEs (1.30) for the dominant mode amplitudes {ak-1, ak0, ak1} decouples from those
for the rest of the modes. The resonant conditions satisfied by the dominant frequencies then allow
us to approximate the dynamics of {ak-1, ak0, ak1} by those described by a resonant 3 × 3 system of
ODEs. The structure of this resonant system (which is sensitive to the precise form of the coefficients
⟨Ek1 ¯Ek2Ek3 ¯Ek⟩in (1.30), which are in turn dependent on the form of the nonlinearity in (1.3) and
our precise choice of the frequency parameters KD) allows us to establish that a non-trivial energy
cascade from the low-frequency mode ˜ϕk0 to the high-frequency pair ˜ϕk-1, ˜ϕk1 takes place at a timescale
T ∼L2s+1. In particular, at that timescale, the sum P
k∈KD ˜ϕk exhibits norm inflation in Hs.
3. Exploiting the quantitative resonant “separation” between the dominant and non-dominant modes, we
show that the non-dominant amplitudes {ak}k∈KND remain small for t ∈[0, T].
4. Finally, we show that the error term ψ remains small in Hs for t ∈[0, T] via an energy estimate based
on a delicate commutation scheme.
In the remainder of this section, we will highlight the technical challenges arising in each of the steps
above.

the mode system (1.30) is motivated by our need to address certain difficulties regarding the control of the
subdominant terms in (1.28):

In Section 5.2, we show that the slowly oscillating approximation is quantitatively accurate on the
time-scale of interest. More precisely, Proposition 5.12 establishes that, for any fixed ˜T > 0, if L is chosen
sufficiently large, then the solution ˜bk(˜t), k ∈{−1, 0, 1}, of the full renormalized system (1.39) arising from
suitable initial data (which are “consistent” with the slowly oscillating ansatz) exists on ˜t ∈[0, ˜T] and remains
O(L−2s−2)-close to the solution bsl
k (˜t), k ∈{−1, 0, 1}, of the slowly oscillating system (1.40).

Remark 1.16 (Spatial resonances and the r−6 weight). Noting that the eigenvalues of the matrix eM in (1.41)
depend crucially on the values of the spatial overlap integrals entering the spectral coefficients of Section 5.1 ,
it is the key inequality (5.29) which implies (via Lemma 5.18) that eM has eigenvalues with strictly positive
real part, thus showing the instability of the dominant mode system. One can check that, in the model where
one drops completely the r−6 weight from the right hand side of (1.4), the spectral coefficients (which can be
computed in a similar fashion) now yield a reduced matrix eM with purely imaginary spectrum, hence the
resonant system in that case is (asymptotically) stable at the linearized level (despite the resonant structure
of the spectrum being identical to our case).

Our argument for obtaining energy estimates for ψ relies, instead, on using commutators which are
adapted both to the geometry of the Schwarzschild–AdS exterior and to the symmetry properties of the
approximate solution ˜ϕ. The helical Killing field
V .= (1 + L−1)∂t + ∂φ
is timelike for r > 2M, extends smoothly to the conformal boundary I = {r = +∞}, and becomes null
there as L →∞. From the point of view of the conformal compactification, V generates a “helical” flow
tangent to the family of null geodesics along which the high-frequency modes of ˜ϕ are concentrated. A key
ingredient is that, for k ∈KD, the eigenfunctions Ek are localized near the equatorial null geodesics at I,
and the corresponding modes satisfy

1.5.7
Completion of the proof of Theorem 1
In Section 8, we assemble the above ingredients in order to conclude the statement of Theorem 1. For any
frequency scale L ≫1 and any Schwarzschild–AdS mass M > 0 for which the spectrum of the corresponding
linear operator possesses a frequency triad (with the properties outlined in Section 4.1) satisfying the resonance
conditions (1.36) and (1.37), the analysis of Sections 5 to 7 applies and yields the growth of the Hs-energy of
the solution given by the ansatz (1.28) on the time interval [0, T1]. In Section 4, we show that these values of
the Schwarzschild–AdS mass are Lδ0-dense in (0, +∞). In particular, the statement of Theorem 1 holds for a
dense set of masses M > 0.
The openness of the same set follows from a soft Cauchy stability argument in the mass parameter: For
fixed initial data and fixed T1, the initial-boundary value problem (1.3) depends continuously on the mass M
in the space C0([0, T1], Hs(Σ∗
t∗) × Hs−1(Σ∗
t∗)); thus, if for some M0, the conclusion of Theorem 1 holds, then
it also holds for all M sufficiently close to M0. Consequently, the conclusion of Theorem 1 holds for an open
and dense set of mass parameters M > 0.
1.6
Norm inflation on the hemisphere: The proof of Theorem 2
In this section, we will outline how the proof of Theorem 1 can be extended to provide a norm inflation
statement for nonlinear equations of the type (1.3) on a broader class of spacetimes exhibiting stable trapping;
a special application of this extension will be Theorem 2. First, let us see how to recast Theorem 1 as a
statement on the spatially compactified domain R × S3

Remark 1.17. In a similar fashion, our proof of Theorem 1 can also be applied to the case of a Kerr–AdS
exterior spacetime (M(a,M)
ext
, ga,M) with a fixed small value of a. Even though these spacetimes are not
spherically symmetric, the wave operator □ga,M separates, and, for modes with ℓ= |m| ≫1, the corresponding
radial ODE has the same asymptotic structure near infinity as in our case, thus allowing one to repeat the
same estimates as the ones we employ for the proof of Theorem 1. In that case, the proof of energy estimates
for the error term ψ follows by using the Hawking vector field rather than the ∂t vector field.

2.1.2
The event horizon.
The spacetime (Mext, gM) can be isometrically embedded into a larger smooth spacetime, which extends Mext
beyond {r = r+}. The “largest” such extension (M, gM) is known as the maximally extended Schwarzschild–
AdS spacetime: this is a black hole spacetime, with Mext being one connected component of the black hole
exterior.
In this paper, we will study functions and tensors on (Mext, gM) which are smooth up to the boundary
∂Mext of Mext inside M. In order to address questions of regularity near ∂Mext, we will have to work in a
coordinate system on Mext which extends smoothly beyond ∂Mext. An example of such a coordinate system
is the double null system (U, V, θ, φ) with
U = −e−(t+y),
V = et−y
(with y = y(r) defined by (2.1)). In these coordinates, gM takes the form
gM =

Note that the coefficients of gM in the above coordinates can be smoothly extended across {U = 0}
and {V = 0}; in this extension, Mext can be identified with (a connected component of) the domain
{U < 0, V > 0}, while ∂Mext (on which r = r+) can be written as the union of two smooth null hypersurfaces
(with boundary)
∂Mext = H+ ∪H−.
In the above, H+ = {U = 0} ∩∂Mext and H−= {V = 0} ∩∂Mext called the future and past event horizons,
respectively. The 2-sphere H+ ∩H−is known as the bifurcation sphere. We will also denote with Mext the
topological closure of Mext in this extended spacetime, i.e.
Mext .= Mext ∪H+ ∪H−.

2.5
Special vector fields on the Schwarzschild–AdS exterior
In Section 7, we will establish a number of a priori energy estimates for the initial–boundary value problem
(7.1) using three distinguished vector fields on Mext as multipliers and commutators: The Killing vector field
T (i.e. the vector field ∂t∗in the (t∗, r, θ, φ) coordinate system), the red-shift vector field Y and the helical
vector field V . We will now proceed to define the vector fields Y and V and describe their basic properties in
more detail. We will also reexpress the conformal wave operator □g + 2 using the above vector fields.

The helical vector field V

Note that V satisfies the following properties:

• In the conformally compactified picture (i.e. in the (t∗, y, θ, φ) coordinate system), V extends smoothly

Remark 2.12. The properties listed above imply that V has “good” commutation properties with respect to
both the conformal wave operator □g + 2 and operators with coefficients which are functions of wave-packets
of frequency ≲L concentrated around null geodesics on I. For this reason, V will be crucial (in the role of a
commutation vector field) in deriving the energy estimates of Section 7.

2.9
Notations on constants and inequalities
We will adopt the standard convention regarding the inequality symbols ≲, ∼and O(·): For any real valued
functions f1, f2 > 0, the expression f1 ≲f2 will be used to denote that there exists a constant C > 0 such
that f1 ≤Cf2, while f1 ∼f2 and f1 = O(f2) will be used to denote, respectively, that f2 ≲f1 ≲f2 and
|f1| ≲f2 (in the latter case, we will allow f1 to be complex valued).
Throughout this work, it will be crucial to keep track of the dependence of the constants implicit in the
≲, ∼, O(·) notation on the various parameters introduced in the proof of Theorem 1 (such as ϵ, s, N, λ and
L). It will always be stated clearly (usually at the beginning of the proof of each lemma, proposition, etc.)
on which parameters those constants are allowed to depend. If no such specification is provided, it will be
assumed that those constants depend only on the geometry of (Mext, gM). With respect to the latter, we
will assume that the dependence of all constants on the Schwarzschild–AdS mass parameter M ∈(0, +∞)
(the only parameter determining the geometry of (Mext, gM)) is uniform over any given pre-compact
subset of (0, +∞) (but might “degenerate” in the limit as M approaches 0 or +∞), unless otherwise stated.
In Section 3 to Section 6, the dependence of our constants on M will be uniform on pre-compact subsets of
[0, +∞) (i.e. they will not degenerate in the limit M →0).

mirror placed at {r = rmirror} (where rmirror = rmirror(M; M0) was defined by (2.8)), namely:

Proof. The proof of Lemma 3.5 will consist of two consecutive bootstrap arguments to deduce the approxi-
mation estimate (3.25) (the bounds (3.22)–(3.24) then following readily by applying Lemma 3.4): At first,
we establish estimate (3.25) for the eigenvalues of an auxiliary Sturm–Liouville problem, corresponding to
the construction of normal mode solutions to the conformally coupled wave equation on pure AdS spacetime
(i.e. with M = 0) with a reflecting mirror placed at some finite radius around r = 0; we will use a continuity
argument with respect to the mirror radius. In the second step, we will obtain (3.25) via a continuity
argument on the Schwarzschild–AdS mass parameter M.
Remark 3.6. For this proof, we will assume that the constants implicit in the O(·) and ≲notation are
independent of n, ℓ(but are allowed to depend on M0, δ).

4
Construction of the solution ϕ
In this section, we will introduce the ansatz that we will use for the function ϕ appearing in the statement
of Theorem 1. This ansatz (see (4.3) below) will be built using the normal mode solutions of the initial–
boundary value problem (3.1) with an inner mirror; it will consist of a triad of dominant modes with frequency
parameters satisfying a set of resonance conditions, a larger set of non-dominant modes which are resonantly
separated from the dominant ones, plus an additional error term. Enforcing those (non-)resonance conditions
will necessitate choosing the Schwarzschild–AdS mass parameter M to lie in a carefully designed dense set
Sϵ,s ⊂(0, +∞). The analysis of the dynamics of the modes and the error term will constitute the bulk of this
paper and will occupy Sections 5 to 7.
Remark 4.1. For the rest of this section, we will assume that we are given a constant M0 > 0 and that
the mass parameter M takes values in (0, M0]. Given such M0 > 0 and M ∈(0, M0], the mirror parameters
rmirror and ymirror will be fixed according to Definition 2.6.

Note that, in the regime when the ak’s are small (in an appropriate sense), the system (4.7) can be
thought of as a perturbation of the linear ODE (3.9) satisfied by the amplitude of a normal mode
solution of the linear boundary value problem (3.1). In particular, in this case, the function ˜ϕk can
be thought of as a mode solution of (3.1) multiplied by a slowly-varying in time amplitude. For this
reason, through a slight abuse of terminology, we will refer to the functions ˜ϕk as mode solutions; the
functions {˜ϕk}k∈KD will be called the dominant modes, while {˜ϕk}k∈K\KD will constitute the set of
non-dominant modes. By a further abuse of terminology, we will frequently also refer to the amplitudes
ak simply as modes.
• The system (4.7) for the ak’s decouples in the following sense: The dominant modes satisfy a 3 × 3
ODE system (which will be analyzed in Section 5), while the non-dominant modes satisfy a decoupled
system of inhomogeneous linear ODEs, with a source term that depends only on the dominant modes
(for the analysis of this system, see Section 6).
• Our choice of the terms dominant modes, non-dominant modes etc. is based on the expected dynamic
behavior of these functions: In the next sections, we will ensure (via an appropriate choice of the
parameters N, λ, L, a perturbation of the mass parameter M and a careful choice of the corresponding
initial data for the ˜ϕk’s) that the dominant modes {˜ϕk}k∈KD contribute the most to the Hs size of the
true solution ϕ, while {˜ϕk}k∈K\KD and ψ remain relatively small.

appropriately, the solution to the corresponding

larger than 1, the system (5.30) would be linearly orbitally stable, and it would not be a priori clear whether

5.2
The slowly oscillating approximation
In this section, we will study the effective dynamics of solutions to (5.5) which oscillate “slowly” with
time (i.e. at a timescale large compared to L−1). For such solutions, we expect that their dynamics are
not affected by the terms in the equations (5.5) with coefficients that either decay with L as L →+∞or
oscillate with frequency ≳L−1; the latter ones are precisely the oscillating terms eiL2s+2(˜ωk−˜ωj1+˜ωj2−˜ωj3)
with |˜ωk −˜ωj1 + ˜ωj2 −˜ωj3| ≳1 (note that the only combinations of k, ji for which the last expression is 0 have
to yield an expression which is a multiple of ˜ω−1 −2˜ω0 + ˜ω1 = 0). In particular, if we formally drop all such
terms from (5.5) (together with all the terms for which the coefficient ⟨⟨¯EkEj1 ¯Ej2Ej3⟩⟩vanishes identically),
we obtain the following 1st order system of ODEs (which we will call the slowly oscillating approximation of
(5.5)):

The proof of Proposition 7.1 will be established via a bootstrap argument; see Section 7.8. In Section 7.1–
Section 7.5, we will set up the commutation scheme and the norms that will be involved in the bootstrap
argument; the main technical tool for the proof of Proposition 7.1 is the a priori energy estimate established
in Section 7.6.
Remark 7.2. Let us note that (7.2) cannot be inferred by only relying on energy estimates using ∂t∗as a
commutator and multiplier; this can be seen by noting that in the linearized version of equation (7.1), namely
□gψ + 2ψ −r−6χ2|˜ϕ|2∂2
t∗ψ −χ2N (1)[˜ϕ; ψ] = −F[˜ϕ],
the oscillations in time of the coefficients involving ˜ϕ are in principle consistent with exponential growth at a
timescale much smaller than T1. The structure that we need to exploit is that ˜ϕ behaves like a wave-packet
localized in phase space around null geodesics at the conformal boundary at r = ∞. In particular, the helical
Killing vector field V defined by (2.16) (and which is timelike away from H+ and degenerates to an almost
null vector field on the conformal boundary at r = ∞) commutes well with ˜ϕ, in the sense that near y = 0,
we have schematically

that any additional differentiation with respect to V will result in a quantity satisfying an estimate which is
worse by an additional factor of L). We will make use of this observation while setting up a commutation
scheme that will allow us to close energy estimates for (7.1).
Remark 7.3. For the rest of this section, we will assume that the constants implicit in the ≲and O(·)

In this section, we will derive the degenerate energy identity for f associated to the Killing vector field ∂t∗,

7.5.1
Coercivity properties of the energy fluxes
We will now study the coercivity properties of the energy fluxes associated to the vector fields ∂t∗and Y (see
Section 2.4.2 for the definition of the notion of the energy flux associated to a vector field).

7.5.2

estimates for solutions to (7.24).

7.7
Estimates for the ˜ϕ terms in the commuted equations for ψ
In this section, we will establish a number of estimates for the terms involving the approximate solution
˜ϕ = P
k∈K ˜ϕk appearing in equations (7.22)–(7.23). These estimates will be used in combination with the a
priori energy bounds of Section 7.6 in the context of our bootstrap argument in the next section. To this end,
we will make use of all the bounds established for the modes {˜ϕk}k∈K in Sections 5 and 6.

Putting everything together: Proof of Theorem 1
In this section, we will combine the results established so far in this paper in order to obtain the proof of
Theorem 1. Let us note that, as a trivial consequence of the well-posedness of the initial-boundary value
problem (1.3) in the class of smooth initial data (see e.g. [50]) and the fact that the coefficients of (1.3)
depend smoothly on the Schwarzschild–AdS mass parameter M, the set of mass parameters M ∈(0, +∞) for
which a solution ϕ of (1.3) exists satisfying the conditions of Theorem 1 is an open subset of (0, +∞). More
precisely, the following holds:
Cauchy stability with respect to the mass parameter: Suppose that, for some ϵ > 0, s ≥4,
T1 > 0 and some value M0 ∈(0, +∞) of the mass parameter, there exists a smooth initial data set
f0, f1 : (r+(M0), +∞) × S2 →C satisfying
suppf0, suppf1 ⊂{r > 3M0}

ext , gM0) with initial data

ϵ .

Then, there exists a δ = δ(ϵ, s, M0, T1, f0, f1) > 0 sufficiently small such that, for any M ∈(M0 −δ, M0 + δ),
the same initial data set (f0, f1) (which can be thought of as a pair of functions on (r+(M), +∞) × S2,

corresponding solution ϕ(M) of (1.3) on (M(M)

As a result, in order to establish Theorem 1, it suffices to show that the set of mass parameters Jϵ,s

Remark A.5. The above is in some sense the most fundamental asymptotic formula for Hermite functions
and its derivation is via a simple application of the WKB method; the bounds of the error term Eν(z) are
of order O(n−1) (i.e. O(ν−2)) relative to the size of the dominant term involving Ai(·) in the expansion.
Utilizing a “higher order” WKB analysis (as described, for instance in [63]), one can in fact derive more
precise asymptotic formulas (i.e. with error terms of smaller relative size); however, the above result will
suffice for the purposes of this paper (namely estimating the size of the Radial eigenfunctions Rn,ℓdefined in
Section 3).
